| [Alabama](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2026_United_States_House_of_Representatives_elections#Alabama) | New districts enacted | In April 2026, the Supreme Court ruled on _[Louisiana v. Callais](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Louisiana_v._Callais "Louisiana v. Callais")_ that stricter scrutiny had to be applied with regard to section 2 of the [Voting Rights Act](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Voting_Rights_Act "Voting Rights Act"). Governor [Kay Ivey](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kay_Ivey "Kay Ivey") was initially reluctant to call a special session after _[Allen v. Milligan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Allen_v._Milligan "Allen v. Milligan")_, but was lobbied to do so by the state attorney general and secretary of state, both of whom also filed emergency injunctions to lift the requirements levied on them by the federal courts.[\[115\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2026_United_States_House_of_Representatives_elections#cite_note-126)<br>The Supreme Court voted 6–3 to lift the stay, paving the way for state legislators to redraw.[\[116\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2026_United_States_House_of_Representatives_elections#cite_note-127) On May 26, 2026, an Alabama court blocked the new map, arguing that the _[Louisiana v. Callais](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Louisiana_v._Callais "Louisiana v. Callais")_ decision does not change the fact that the map intentionally discriminates against African-American voters in violation of the Constitution.[\[117\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2026_United_States_House_of_Representatives_elections#cite_note-128) On June 2, 2026, the [Supreme Court](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Supreme_Court_of_the_United_States "Supreme Court of the United States") granted stay on the block, allowing the new map to be implemented.[\[118\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2026_United_States_House_of_Representatives_elections#cite_note-129) | ![Steady](https://thumb.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/9/96/Steady2.svg/20px-Steady2.svg.png?utm_source=en.wikipedia.org&utm_campaign=parser&utm_content=thumbnail) | ![Decrease](https://thumb.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/e/ed/Decrease2.svg/20px-Decrease2.svg.png?utm_source=en.wikipedia.org&utm_campaign=parser&utm_content=thumbnail) 1 | ![Increase](https://thumb.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/b/b0/Increase2.svg/20px-Increase2.svg.png?utm_source=en.wikipedia.org&utm_campaign=parser&utm_content=thumbnail) 1 |

Thurston_, plaintiffs alleged racial gerrymandering diluting the voting power of black voters by splitting [Little Rock](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Little_Rock "Little Rock") into three districts. The case was heard in a federal district court. The panel ruled that it did not constitute a racial gerrymander and dismissed the case.[\[119\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2026_United_States_House_of_Representatives_elections#cite_note-130) | ![Steady](https://thumb.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/9/96/Steady2.svg/20px-Steady2.svg.png?utm_source=en.wikipedia.org&utm_campaign=parser&utm_content=thumbnail) | ![Steady](https://thumb.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/9/96/Steady2.svg/20px-Steady2.svg.png?utm_source=en.wikipedia.org&utm_campaign=parser&utm_content=thumbnail) | ![Steady](https://thumb.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/9/96/Steady2.svg/20px-Steady2.svg.png?utm_source=en.wikipedia.org&utm_campaign=parser&utm_content=thumbnail) |
| [California](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2026_United_States_House_of_Representatives_elections#California) | [New districts enacted](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2025_California_proposed_redistricting "2025 California proposed redistricting") | In response to Texas's redistricting, a new map was approved by voters in a special statewide election temporarily permitting an off-cycle redistricting. The state's [constitution](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Constitution_of_California "Constitution of California") prohibits the [state legislature](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/California_State_Legislature "California State Legislature") from drawing congressional districts, so the map was subject to a 2025 referendum. It was passed by two-thirds of the legislature and over a 64% majority approval by the state's voters for the new districts to take place.[\[120\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2026_United_States_House_of_Representatives_elections#cite_note-CM-131) | ![Increase](https://thumb.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/b/b0/Increase2.svg/20px-Increase2.svg.png?utm_source=en.wikipedia.org&utm_campaign=parser&utm_content=thumbnail) 4 | ![Decrease](https://thumb.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/e/ed/Decrease2.svg/20px-Decrease2.svg.png?utm_source=en.wikipedia.org&utm_campaign=parser&utm_content=thumbnail) 3 | ![Decrease](https://thumb.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/e/ed/Decrease2.svg/20px-Decrease2.svg.png?utm_source=en.wikipedia.org&utm_campaign=parser&utm_content=thumbnail) 1 |
| [Florida](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2026_United_States_House_of_Representatives_elections#Florida) | [New districts enacted](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2026_Florida_redistricting "2026 Florida redistricting") | In September 2023, a state circuit judge ruled that the map passed by the state legislature discriminated against Black voters, by redrawing the formerly plurality-Black 5th district to no longer have a Black plurality. Florida appealed this ruling and the map was reinstated by an appeals court.[\[121\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2026_United_States_House_of_Representatives_elections#cite_note-132) In July 2025, the Florida Supreme Court ruled 5–1 to keep the current maps in place, finding that creating a plurality-Black congressional district would violate the federal [Equal Protection Clause](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Equal_Protection_Clause "Equal Protection Clause").[\[122\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2026_United_States_House_of_Representatives_elections#cite_note-133)<br>On January 9, 2026, Governor [Ron DeSantis](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ron_DeSantis "Ron DeSantis") called for a special session in April redrawing the state's congressional maps with the target of gaining 3 to 5 new Republican seats. However, Florida state law prevents deliberately redrawing congressional maps purely for partisan gain.[\[123\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2026_United_States_House_of_Representatives_elections#cite_note-134) On February 27, 2026, the [Supreme Court of Florida](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Supreme_Court_of_Florida "Supreme Court of Florida") rejected a petition to block the special session.[\[124\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2026_United_States_House_of_Representatives_elections#cite_note-135) On April 29, 2026, the maps were approved by the [Florida Legislature](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Florida_Legislature "Florida Legislature").[\[125\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2026_United_States_House_of_Representatives_elections#cite_note-136) On May 4, 2026, Governor DeSantis signed the new map into law.[\[126\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2026_United_States_House_of_Representatives_elections#cite_note-137) | ![Increase](https://thumb.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/b/b0/Increase2.svg/20px-Increase2.svg.png?utm_source=en.wikipedia.org&utm_campaign=parser&utm_content=thumbnail) 1 | ![Decrease](https://thumb.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/e/ed/Decrease2.svg/20px-Decrease2.svg.png?utm_source=en.wikipedia.org&utm_campaign=parser&utm_content=thumbnail) 4 | ![Increase](https://thumb.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/b/b0/Increase2.svg/20px-Increase2.svg.png?utm_source=en.wikipedia.org&utm_campaign=parser&utm_content=thumbnail) 3 |
| [Indiana](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2026_United_States_House_of_Representatives_elections#Indiana) | Districts left in place | On November 18, 2025, the Indiana State Senate voted 29–19 against meeting in December with their House colleagues to discuss redistricting.[\[127\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2026_United_States_House_of_Representatives_elections#cite_note-138) Despite this, Governor [Mike Braun](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mike_Braun "Mike Braun") stated that he would "explore all options" to enable redistricting, in response to pressure from President Trump. On November 25, the state senate announced it would vote on a potential proposal approved by the state house.[\[128\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2026_United_States_House_of_Representatives_elections#cite_note-139) After passing in the state house, the state senate rejected the proposal 31–19.[\[108\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2026_United_States_House_of_Representatives_elections#cite_note-INmap-118) | ![Steady](https://thumb.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/9/96/Steady2.svg/20px-Steady2.svg.png?utm_source=en.wikipedia.org&utm_campaign=parser&utm_content=thumbnail) | ![Steady](https://thumb.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/9/96/Steady2.svg/20px-Steady2.svg.png?utm_source=en.wikipedia.org&utm_campaign=parser&utm_content=thumbnail) | ![Steady](https://thumb.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/9/96/Steady2.svg/20px-Steady2.svg.png?utm_source=en.wikipedia.org&utm_campaign=parser&utm_content=thumbnail) |

| [Louisiana](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2026_United_States_House_of_Representatives_elections#Louisiana) | New districts enacted | In April 2026, the Supreme Court ruled on _[Louisiana v. Callais](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Louisiana_v._Callais "Louisiana v. Callais")_ that stricter scrutiny had to be applied with regard to section 2 of the [Voting Rights Act](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Voting_Rights_Act "Voting Rights Act"). The state following the ruling requested the Court strike down the lower court ruling to have two black majority districts, which it proceeded to do. The state government has pushed to move back the primary dates in light of the court requiring redistricting.[\[129\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2026_United_States_House_of_Representatives_elections#cite_note-140) On May 29, 2026, Governor [Jeff Landry](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jeff_Landry "Jeff Landry") signed a new map into law, removing one of the two plurality-Black, Democratic-controlled districts.[\[130\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2026_United_States_House_of_Representatives_elections#cite_note-141) | ![Decrease](https://thumb.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/e/ed/Decrease2.svg/20px-Decrease2.svg.png?utm_source=en.wikipedia.org&utm_campaign=parser&utm_content=thumbnail) 1 | ![Steady](https://thumb.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/9/96/Steady2.svg/20px-Steady2.svg.png?utm_source=en.wikipedia.org&utm_campaign=parser&utm_content=thumbnail) | ![Increase](https://thumb.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/b/b0/Increase2.svg/20px-Increase2.svg.png?utm_source=en.wikipedia.org&utm_campaign=parser&utm_content=thumbnail) 1 |
| [Maryland](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2026_United_States_House_of_Representatives_elections#Maryland) | Districts left in place | In November 2025, Governor [Wes Moore](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wes_Moore "Wes Moore") announced the creation of the Redistricting Advisory Commission which began the process of redistricting the congressional lines to make the 1st district more Democratic. The plan has received support from State House Speaker [Joseline Peña-Melnyk](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Joseline_Pe%C3%B1a-Melnyk "Joseline Peña-Melnyk"), but opposition from State Senate President [Bill Ferguson](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bill_Ferguson_(politician) "Bill Ferguson (politician)"). In January 2026, the Governor's Redistricting Advisory Commission voted to recommend a congressional map that would redraw the 1st congressional district to make it more favorable for Democrats.[\[131\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2026_United_States_House_of_Representatives_elections#cite_note-142) The map passed the state house on February 2.[\[111\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2026_United_States_House_of_Representatives_elections#cite_note-MDmap-121) On February 20, Ferguson told reporters that the window to redraw Maryland's congressional maps had closed, killing the bill.[\[132\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2026_United_States_House_of_Representatives_elections#cite_note-143) | ![Steady](https://thumb.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/9/96/Steady2.svg/20px-Steady2.svg.png?utm_source=en.wikipedia.org&utm_campaign=parser&utm_content=thumbnail) | ![Steady](https://thumb.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/9/96/Steady2.svg/20px-Steady2.svg.png?utm_source=en.wikipedia.org&utm_campaign=parser&utm_content=thumbnail) | ![Steady](https://thumb.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/9/96/Steady2.svg/20px-Steady2.svg.png?utm_source=en.wikipedia.org&utm_campaign=parser&utm_content=thumbnail) |
| [Missouri](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2026_United_States_House_of_Representatives_elections#Missouri) | [Districts left in place](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2025_Missouri_redistricting "2025 Missouri redistricting") | The [Missouri Legislature](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Missouri_Legislature "Missouri Legislature") passed maps to create a new safely Republican seat in the [5th district](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Missouri's_5th_congressional_district "Missouri's 5th congressional district"). Governor [Mike Kehoe](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mike_Kehoe "Mike Kehoe") signed the map into law in September 2025.[\[103\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2026_United_States_House_of_Representatives_elections#cite_note-:1-113) The maps may be blocked by a referendum if enough signatures are gathered.[\[133\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2026_United_States_House_of_Representatives_elections#cite_note-144) In March 2026, opponents of the map said they had enough signatures for the referendum to qualify.[\[134\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2026_United_States_House_of_Representatives_elections#cite_note-Signatures-145)<br>On August 4, 2026, Missouri Secretary of State [Denny Hoskins](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Denny_Hoskins "Denny Hoskins") blocked the referendum from taking place, arguing that the referendum would be unconstitutional under [Missouri's Constitution](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Constitution_of_Missouri "Constitution of Missouri"),[\[135\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2026_United_States_House_of_Representatives_elections#cite_note-146) a decision which was upheld by [Cole County](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cole_County,_Missouri "Cole County, Missouri") Circuit Judge Daniel Green on August 19.[\[136\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2026_United_States_House_of_Representatives_elections#cite_note-147) However, on September 3, the [Missouri Supreme Court](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Missouri_Supreme_Court "Missouri Supreme Court") unanimously ruled that the referendum is sufficient and must be put on the ballot, thus leaving the 2022 maps in place for the 2026 elections.[\[104\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2026_United_States_House_of_Representatives_elections#cite_note-:6-114) On September 8, U.S. Supreme Court Justice [Brett Kavanaugh](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Brett_Kavanaugh "Brett Kavanaugh") denied an emergency appeal by State Republicans.[\[137\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2026_United_States_House_of_Representatives_elections#cite_note-148)[\[138\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2026_United_States_House_of_Representatives_elections#cite_note-149) However, the same day, Chief U.S. District Judge [Stephen Clark](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Stephen_R._Clark "Stephen R. Clark") of the [Eastern District of Missouri](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/United_States_District_Court_for_the_Eastern_District_of_Missouri "United States District Court for the Eastern District of Missouri") issued a [temporary restraining order](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Temporary_restraining_order "Temporary restraining order") in a separate lawsuit prohibiting the state from using anything but the new 2025 maps.[\[139\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2026_United_States_House_of_Representatives_elections#cite_note-150) On September 10, the U.S. Supreme Court granted an emergency stay of Judge Clark's order, putting the 2022 map back into place for the 2026 elections.[\[140\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2026_United_States_House_of_Representatives_elections#cite_note-151) However, on September 21, the [United States Court of Appeals for the Eighth Circuit](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/United_States_Court_of_Appeals_for_the_Eighth_Circuit "United States Court of Appeals for the Eighth Circuit"), ruling on the merits, affirmed the temporary restraining order and remanded the case to the lower court for entry of a [permanent injunction](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Permanent_injunction "Permanent injunction") enjoining Missouri to use the 2025 map, but stayed their ruling for a week pending appeal to the U.S. Supreme Court.[\[141\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2026_United_States_House_of_Representatives_elections#cite_note-152) On September 25, the U.S. Supreme Court unanimously held that state law rules in the case and granted an emergency stay of the Eighth Circuit's order and Judge Clark's injunction, leaving the 2022 maps in place for the 2026 elections.[\[142\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2026_United_States_House_of_Representatives_elections#cite_note-153) | ![Steady](https://thumb.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/9/96/Steady2.svg/20px-Steady2.svg.png?utm_source=en.wikipedia.org&utm_campaign=parser&utm_content=thumbnail) | | [New York](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2026_United_States_House_of_Representatives_elections#New_York) | Districts left in place | Voters in [NY-11](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/New_York's_11th_congressional_district "New York's 11th congressional district") filed a lawsuit in October 2025, claiming the Staten Island-based district illegally dilutes the power of Black and Hispanic voters in the district.[\[143\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2026_United_States_House_of_Representatives_elections#cite_note-154) Acting [New York Supreme Court](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/New_York_Supreme_Court "New York Supreme Court") Justice [Jeffrey Pearlman](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jeffrey_Pearlman "Jeffrey Pearlman") ordered the [independent redistricting commission](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Independent_redistricting_commission "Independent redistricting commission") to make new maps to reconfigure NY-11 by February 6, 2026.[\[144\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2026_United_States_House_of_Representatives_elections#cite_note-NYMap-155) The deadline was suspended after [Rep. Malliotakis](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nicole_Malliotakis "Nicole Malliotakis"), who represents the district, appealed the ruling.[\[145\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2026_United_States_House_of_Representatives_elections#cite_note-156) On March 2, the U.S. Supreme Court temporarily blocked Pearlman's order, allowing the state's current map to be used for the 2026 election.[\[146\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2026_United_States_House_of_Representatives_elections#cite_note-157) | ![Steady](https://thumb.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/9/96/Steady2.svg/20px-Steady2.svg.png?utm_source=en.wikipedia.org&utm_campaign=parser&utm_content=thumbnail) | ![Steady](https://thumb.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/9/96/Steady2.svg/20px-Steady2.svg.png?utm_source=en.wikipedia.org&utm_campaign=parser&utm_content=thumbnail) | ![Steady](https://thumb.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/9/96/Steady2.svg/20px-Steady2.svg.png?utm_source=en.wikipedia.org&utm_campaign=parser&utm_content=thumbnail) |
| [North Carolina](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2026_United_States_House_of_Representatives_elections#North_Carolina) | [New districts enacted](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2025_North_Carolina_redistricting "2025 North Carolina redistricting") | The state Senate passed the bill for a new map making the competitive 1st congressional district more Republican-leaning on October 21, 2025. The state House passed it the next day. The governor, Josh Stein, does not have the authority to veto the map.[\[105\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2026_United_States_House_of_Representatives_elections#cite_note-NCmap-115) It has received pushback from the [NAACP](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/NAACP "NAACP") and [Common Cause](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Common_Cause "Common Cause"), both of which accused the new map of diluting the minority vote. A hearing was held on November 19 to consider blocking the map.[\[147\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2026_United_States_House_of_Representatives_elections#cite_note-158) | ![Steady](https://thumb.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/9/96/Steady2.svg/20px-Steady2.svg.png?utm_source=en.wikipedia.org&utm_campaign=parser&utm_content=thumbnail) | ![Decrease](https://thumb.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/e/ed/Decrease2.svg/20px-Decrease2.svg.png?utm_source=en.wikipedia.org&utm_campaign=parser&utm_content=thumbnail) 1 | ![Increase](https://thumb.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/b/b0/Increase2.svg/20px-Increase2.svg.png?utm_source=en.wikipedia.org&utm_campaign=parser&utm_content=thumbnail) 1 |
| [Ohio](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2026_United_States_House_of_Representatives_elections#Ohio) | New districts enacted | Ohio voters amended the constitution in 2018, creating a process that requires both Republicans and Democrats in the state legislature to vote on the new congressional map. In the 2020 redistricting cycle, Democrats did not back a Republican proposal, necessitating a new map to be drawn in 2025. In October 2025, the Ohio Redistricting Commission passed a compromise map in a unanimous vote. The new map makes the 1st district more competitive, the 9th district lean more Republican, and the 13th district lean more Democratic.[\[98\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2026_United_States_House_of_Representatives_elections#cite_note-OHnewmap-108) | ![Steady](https://thumb.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/9/96/Steady2.svg/20px-Steady2.svg.png?utm_source=en.wikipedia.org&utm_campaign=parser&utm_content=thumbnail) | ![Decrease](https://thumb.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/e/ed/Decrease2.svg/20px-Decrease2.svg.png?utm_source=en.wikipedia.org&utm_campaign=parser&utm_content=thumbnail) 2 | ![Increase](https://thumb.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/b/b0/Increase2.svg/20px-Increase2.svg.png?utm_source=en.wikipedia.org&utm_campaign=parser&utm_content=thumbnail) 2 |
| [South Carolina](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2026_United_States_House_of_Representatives_elections#South_Carolina) | Districts left in place | In June 2025, the [South Carolina Supreme Court](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/South_Carolina_Supreme_Court "South Carolina Supreme Court") heard arguments in _[League of Women Voters v. Alexander](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/League_of_Women_Voters_of_South_Carolina_v._Alexander "League of Women Voters of South Carolina v. Alexander")_, as to whether the congressional maps are an unlawful partisan gerrymander in violation of the [state's constitution](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Constitution_of_South_Carolina "Constitution of South Carolina").[\[148\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2026_United_States_House_of_Representatives_elections#cite_note-gm-159) In September 2025, the court ruled that partisan gerrymandering is not in violation of the state's constitution, repeatedly referencing the 2019 SCOTUS case _[Rucho v. Common Cause](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rucho_v._Common_Cause "Rucho v. Common Cause")_, leaving the current map in place.[\[93\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2026_United_States_House_of_Representatives_elections#cite_note-:0-103)<br>Following the U.S. Supreme Court's decision in _[Louisiana v. Callais](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Louisiana_v._Callais "Louisiana v. Callais")_, Governor [Henry McMaster](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Henry_McMaster "Henry McMaster") called a special legislative session in May 2026 to consider redistricting and the state house approved a new map that would redraw the state's sole majority-minority district. However, the state senate ultimately voted against advancing the map after it became clear that passage would occur after the start of early voting in the state's primary election.[\[149\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2026_United_States_House_of_Representatives_elections#cite_note-160) | ![Steady](https://thumb.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/9/96/Steady2.svg/20px-Steady2.svg.png?utm_source=en.wikipedia.org&utm_campaign=parser&utm_content=thumbnail) | ![Steady](https://thumb.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/9/96/Steady2.svg/20px-Steady2.svg.png?utm_source=en.wikipedia.org&utm_campaign=parser&utm_content=thumbnail) | ![Steady](https://thumb.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/9/96/Steady2.svg/20px-Steady2.svg.png?utm_source=en.wikipedia.org&utm_campaign=parser&utm_content=thumbnail) |
| [Tennessee](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2026_United_States_House_of_Representatives_elections#Tennessee) | [New districts enacted](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2026_Tennessee_redistricting "2026 Tennessee redistricting") | In April 2026, the Supreme Court ruled on _[Louisiana v. Callais](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Louisiana_v._Callais "Louisiana v. Callais")_ that stricter scrutiny had to be applied with regard to section 2 of the [Voting Rights Act](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Voting_Rights_Act "Voting Rights Act"). After facing calls to redistrict from U.S. Senator [Marsha Blackburn](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Marsha_Blackburn "Marsha Blackburn") and U.S. President [Donald Trump](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Donald_Trump "Donald Trump"), Governor [Bill Lee](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bill_Lee_(Tennessee_politician) "Bill Lee (Tennessee politician)") called a special session to redraw the sole majority-minority district in Tennessee.[\[150\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2026_United_States_House_of_Representatives_elections#cite_note-161) | ![Decrease](https://thumb.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/e/ed/Decrease2.svg/20px-Decrease2.svg.png?utm_source=en.wikipedia.org&utm_campaign=parser&utm_content=thumbnail) 1 | ![Steady](https://thumb.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/9/96/Steady2.svg/20px-Steady2.svg.png?utm_source=en.wikipedia.org&utm_campaign=parser&utm_content=thumbnail) | ![Increase](https://thumb.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/b/b0/Increase2.svg/20px-Increase2.svg.png?utm_source=en.wikipedia.org&utm_campaign=parser&utm_content=thumbnail) 1 |
| [Texas](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2026_United_States_House_of_Representatives_elections#Texas) | [New districts enacted](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2025_Texas_redistricting "2025 Texas redistricting") | In _League of United Latin American Citizens v. Abbott_, held from May to June 2025 in the [U.S. District Court for the Western District of Texas](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/United_States_District_Court_for_the_Western_District_of_Texas "United States District Court for the Western District of Texas"), plaintiffs argued that the congressional districts passed by the state legislature discriminated against Black and Latino voters in violation of the Voting Rights Act.[\[151\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2026_United_States_House_of_Representatives_elections#cite_note-162)<br>In early 2025, the Trump Administration urged Texas officials to redraw congressional districts. This was followed by a meeting of the Texas Republican congressional delegation to discuss mid-decade redistricting to secure extra Republican seats.[\[152\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2026_United_States_House_of_Representatives_elections#cite_note-163) On July 9, Governor [Greg Abbott](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Greg_Abbott "Greg Abbott") called a special session of the legislature to be held later that month with the goal of creating 5 new Republican seats. After Texas House Democrats staged a weeks-long walkout, the legislature passed the new maps on August 23, and Abbott signed them into law on August 29.[\[99\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2026_United_States_House_of_Representatives_elections#cite_note-tg-109)<br> The previous case was paused, pending new maps.[\[95\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2026_United_States_House_of_Representatives_elections#cite_note-LULAC-105) After passage, on August 27, the court scheduled a preliminary injunction hearing to begin on October 1 to October 10.[\[153\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2026_United_States_House_of_Representatives_elections#cite_note-oh-164) On November 18, the panel issued a preliminary injunction blocking the new map, putting the former map back in place. The case on the 2021 maps remains open, pending action from the U.S. Supreme Court in _[Louisiana v. Callais](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Louisiana_v._Callais "Louisiana v. Callais")_.[\[100\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2026_United_States_House_of_Representatives_elections#cite_note-:5-110) On November 21, the U.S. Supreme Court issued an administrative stay on the lower court ruling, temporarily leaving the new map in place.[\[101\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2026_United_States_House_of_Representatives_elections#cite_note-TXmap-111) On December 4, the Supreme Court ruled 6–3 in allowing the map to stand for the 2026 elections.[\[154\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2026_United_States_House_of_Representatives_elections#cite_note-165) | ![Decrease](https://thumb.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/e/ed/Decrease2.svg/20px-Decrease2.svg.png?utm_source=en.wikipedia.org&utm_campaign=parser&utm_content=thumbnail) 3 | ![Decrease](https://thumb.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/e/ed/Decrease2.svg/20px-Decrease2.svg.png?utm_source=en.wikipedia.org&utm_campaign=parser&utm_content=thumbnail) 2 | ![Increase](https://thumb.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/b/b0/Increase2.svg/20px-Increase2.svg.png?utm_source=en.wikipedia.org&utm_campaign=parser&utm_content=thumbnail) 5 |
| [Utah](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2026_United_States_House_of_Representatives_elections#Utah) | New districts enacted | In July 2023, in _[League of Women Voters v. Utah State Legislature](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/League_of_Women_Voters_v._Utah_State_Legislature "League of Women Voters v. Utah State Legislature")_, the [Utah Supreme Court](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Utah_Supreme_Court "Utah Supreme Court") heard arguments alleging that the Utah Legislature violated [a citizen-passed anti-gerrymandering proposition](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2018_Utah_Proposition_4 "2018 Utah Proposition 4") by dividing Salt Lake County voters into all four of Utah's districts. The case was sent back to a state district court, which held a hearing in January 2025. A ruling for the League of Women Voters was issued on August 25. New maps were expected to be drawn up by the Legislature by September 24 and finalized by November.[\[155\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2026_United_States_House_of_Representatives_elections#cite_note-redrawn-166)<br>On October 6, the Utah Legislature passed a new map, which changed 2 Republican districts to be more competitive.[\[156\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2026_United_States_House_of_Representatives_elections#cite_note-:2-167) On November 10, Utah Third District Judge Dianna Gibson ruled that the new map also violated Proposition 4, opting to approve a plaintiff-submitted alternative, Map 1, which creates a safe Democratic district comprising only [Salt Lake County](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Salt_Lake_County "Salt Lake County").[\[96\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2026_United_States_House_of_Representatives_elections#cite_note-:4-106) | ![Increase](https://thumb.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/b/b0/Increase2.svg/20px-Increase2.svg.png?utm_source=en.wikipedia.org&utm_campaign=parser&utm_content=thumbnail) 1 | ![Steady](https://thumb.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/9/96/Steady2.svg/20px-Steady2.svg.png?utm_source=en.wikipedia.org&utm_campaign=parser&utm_content=thumbnail) | ![Decrease](https://thumb.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/e/ed/Decrease2.svg/20px-Decrease2.svg.png?utm_source=en.wikipedia.org&utm_campaign=parser&utm_content=thumbnail) 1 |
| [Virginia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2026_United_States_House_of_Representatives_elections#Virginia) | Districts left in place | Democratic state lawmakers announced a plan to alter the state constitution to draw a new congressional map before the 2026 elections making 3–4 more Democratic seats.[\[110\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2026_United_States_House_of_Representatives_elections#cite_note-auto-120)<br>The proposed mechanism was an amendment to the [Constitution of Virginia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Constitution_of_Virginia "Constitution of Virginia") allowing the [General Assembly](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Virginia_General_Assembly "Virginia General Assembly") to modify the state's congressional districts if any other state did so between January 1, 2025, and October 31, 2030, for any reason other than normal decennial redistricting or to satisfy a court order. Redistricting was transferred from the General Assembly to a bipartisan commission in [2020](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2020_Virginia_Question_1 "2020 Virginia Question 1"), and the power would revert to the commission for the normal 2030 cycle. The amendment proposal first passed the General Assembly on October 31, 2025. The amendment procedure requires any proposal to pass in both houses of the General Assembly, then it must pass both houses again after the next [Virginia House of Delegates](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Virginia_House_of_Delegates "Virginia House of Delegates") election is held (every two years), then it must be approved in a referendum.[\[157\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2026_United_States_House_of_Representatives_elections#cite_note-168)<br>[Virginia Senate](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Virginia_Senate "Virginia Senate") minority leader [Ryan McDougle](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ryan_McDougle "Ryan McDougle"), a Republican, sued Democratic House Speaker [Don Scott](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Don_Scott_(Virginia_politician) "Don Scott (Virginia politician)") over the legality of the amendment. On January 27, 2026, [Tazewell County](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tazewell_County,_Virginia "Tazewell County, Virginia") Circuit Court Judge Jack Hurley, Jr. ruled that the amendment could not satisfy the intervening-election requirement since early voting in the [2025 Virginia elections](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2025_Virginia_elections "2025 Virginia elections") was underway at the time of passage.[\[158\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2026_United_States_House_of_Representatives_elections#cite_note-169) On January 28, the [Democratic Party of Virginia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Democratic_Party_of_Virginia "Democratic Party of Virginia") appealed the ruling; the court of appeals, citing the importance of the case, forwarded it to the [Supreme Court of Virginia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Supreme_Court_of_Virginia "Supreme Court of Virginia") for an expedited ruling on February 4.[\[159\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2026_United_States_House_of_Representatives_elections#cite_note-170)[\[160\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2026_United_States_House_of_Representatives_elections#cite_note-171) On February 10, the General Assembly passed the amendment proposal the required second time and included a draft map that would likely net Democrats four seats.[\[161\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2026_United_States_House_of_Representatives_elections#cite_note-172) On February 13, the Supreme Court allowed the April 21 [referendum](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2026_Virginia_redistricting_amendment "2026 Virginia redistricting amendment") to go forward while it considered the matter. The referendum passed with 51.7% voting yes.[\[162\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2026_United_States_House_of_Representatives_elections#cite_note-173) The next day, Judge Hurley ruled the referendum to be unconstitutional on the basis of another lawsuit and enjoined the state board of elections from certifying the result. Attorney General [Jay Jones](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jay_Jones "Jay Jones") appealed the decision; the Supreme Court declined to stay it while the original case was ongoing and as a result the board was unable to certify the result at its May meeting.[\[163\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2026_United_States_House_of_Representatives_elections#cite_note-174)[\[164\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2026_United_States_House_of_Representatives_elections#cite_note-175) The Supreme Court heard oral arguments in the case on April 27.[\[165\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2026_United_States_House_of_Representatives_elections#cite_note-176)<br> <br>On May 8, the Supreme Court invalidated the amendment in a 4–3 decision, with the majority agreeing with Judge Hurley's ruling that its first passage was too late to satisfy the intervening-election requirement.[\[166\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2026_United_States_House_of_Representatives_elections#cite_note-177)<br>Following the ruling, Speaker Scott and Attorney General Jones filed an emergency appeal to the [United States Supreme Court](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/United_States_Supreme_Court "United States Supreme Court").[\[167\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2026_United_States_House_of_Representatives_elections#cite_note-178) On May 15, the Supreme Court denied the appeal.[\[168\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2026_United_States_House_of_Representatives_elections#cite_note-179) | ![Steady](https://thumb.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/9/96/Steady2.svg/20px-Steady2.svg.png?utm_source=en.wikipedia.org&utm_campaign=parser&utm_content=thumbnail) | ![Steady](https://thumb.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/9/96/Steady2.svg/20px-Steady2.svg.png?utm_source=en.wikipedia.org&utm_campaign=parser&utm_content=thumbnail) | ![Steady](https://thumb.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/9/96/Steady2.svg/20px-Steady2.svg.png?utm_source=en.wikipedia.org&utm_campaign=parser&utm_content=thumbnail) |
| [Wisconsin](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2026_United_States_House_of_Representatives_elections#Wisconsin) | Districts left in place | In July 2025, a lawsuit was filed by two liberal law firms in a Wisconsin district court, asking to consider the constitutionality of Wisconsin's congressional maps.[\[169\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2026_United_States_House_of_Representatives_elections#cite_note-180) In September 2025, the Wisconsin Supreme court addressed this lawsuit and asked each party to provide briefs on whether this court case should proceed.[\[170\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2026_United_States_House_of_Representatives_elections#cite_note-181) On March 31, 2026, a judicial panel dismissed the challenge.[\[171\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2026_United_States_House_of_Representatives_elections#cite_note-182) | ![Steady](https://thumb.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/9/96/Steady2.svg/20px-Steady2.svg.png?utm_source=en.wikipedia.org&utm_campaign=parser&utm_content=thumbnail) | ![Steady](https://thumb.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/9/96/Steady2.svg/20px-Steady2.svg.png?utm_source=en.wikipedia.org&utm_campaign=parser&utm_content=thumbnail) | ![Steady](https://thumb.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/9/96/Steady2.svg/20px-Steady2.svg.png?utm_source=en.wikipedia.org&utm_campaign=parser&utm_content=thumbnail) |
